The switchboard made that skill routine, and it made the ARVN's dependence routine too.

### Clock

The combined command's effect on American political tolerance in 1968 was nil to slightly negative, and the task force's was nil. Hue was shorter by five days and cost about 15 fewer American lives¹⁵. That is a trace against 14,592 American dead by hostile action in the year¹, and it did not touch the decision of 31 March. The decision turned on Tet's surprise and on the reinforcement request, and nothing in this memorandum changed either. On the other side of the ledger, an American commander holding operational control of the ARVN made it harder for the administration to say, in 1968, that the war was the South Vietnamese army's to win. Senators who asked why the Republic could not command its own army asked it in the spring of 1968 with a fact to point at. The administration answered with Vietnamization, which it would have announced anyway. I judge the clock unmoved in Phase III by either provision.

## §4.P4 Phase IV — Vietnamization and drawdown (Jan 1969 – Dec 1971)

Neither provision changes an event of Phase IV, for two reasons. The combined command's gains were in fixing the main force and coordinating fire while American divisions were present, and in 1969–71 they left. Lam Son 719 was ARVN-only by law, and no command arrangement lifted that bar. The Phase III end vector moved the infrastructure to −8..−6, but the incoming change does not alter this phase's −4..−2, because that range is measured against history's 1971. History's own Phoenix and Accelerated Pacification closed most of the gap in 1969–71. The task force's American half and its helicopters drew down with the forces, and its file went into the Central Intelligence Organization with memorandum 006's in 1970.

## §4.P5 Phase V — The Easter Offensive and Linebacker (Jan 1972 – Dec 1972)

Neither provision bites in 1972. The Easter Offensive was decided by American air power at An Loc and Kontum and by ARVN generalship at Quang Tri. The combined switchboard's skill was already in the record through the educated staffs and the advisers. The combined command's American divisions were gone, and it could not choose corps commanders. The task force's targets were the infrastructure, and the offensive was a conventional invasion. Where PAVN divisions entered the peopled lowlands, the gains of 1964–71 were lost to divisions, not cadres.

## §4.P6 Phase VI — Paris, the endgame, and 1975 (Jan 1973 – Dec 1975)

Neither provision changes the last phase's events, and the reason is the critical path of 1975. The collapse of I and II Corps was set by the palace's orders, the soldiers' families and the PAVN's roads, and no command arrangement withdrawn in 1973 reaches those. The task force's mark is already in the record. The Front's committees in the held belt lost about twice the historical share of their members in 1973, on files ten years deep. The national file is in Saigon at the end of 1975, uncaptured, because Saigon stands.

## §4.H Historical comparison

Against history, the cumulative force of twelve memoranda has changed the war's countryside deeply, its main-force war moderately and its end by about a year. This memorandum's share of that change is concentrated in three places.

**The infrastructure in the Saigon belt and the upper Delta, 1964–68.** History had no targeted attack on the infrastructure before ICEX in mid-1967, and Phoenix proper began in 1968¹⁶. The record had already given the war a police and district centres from 1964. This memorandum gives it a finishing arm from the same date. Its measurable result is in the enemy's own accounts. The Saigon–Gia Dinh Region's collections from the posted villages of Hoc Mon and Go Vap fell to about an eighth of 1964's by the first half of 1966, against about a fifth on the course before this memorandum, and the Front governed Hoc Mon from the Ho Bo woods rather than from Cu Chi. The Can Giuoc security section that killed 37 title holders in 1964 was broken by December of that year, and no title holder was killed there in 1965. I judge the Front's district-level administration in the held belt and its edge to be about a third to a half weaker at the end of 1967 than the record held without this memorandum, in about 12–18 of the 44 provinces¹⁵. That is a margin over history of about four years and a large fraction of the district layer, in the part of the country that held longest in 1975.

**One battle.** Hue ended on 27 February rather than 2 March, the flag came down on 19 February rather than the 24th, and about 40 ARVN and 15 American lives were saved by my estimate¹⁵. The city's civilian dead are history's, less a few hundred from the shelling of the western quarter at most.

**The command of the war.** For the first time, the war had one commander for the military campaign in the South from 1964: an American, with a Vietnamese deputy who countersigned through the prime minister's office, a Korean veto and an Australian casualty referral. The gap register's thirteenth gap, no single manager of the war, is narrowed for the military war and untouched for pacification, which CORDS still took over on its historical date. The combined command closed seams between national sectors, made Hue one battle, and began a transfer in the Delta that stopped at the palace's choice of corps commander.

**What history kept.** The main force was found no better, chose its battles as before, and regenerated in sanctuaries no provision touched. The coastal lowlands of I and II Corps, where the files were ralliers' words, got a list, a killed family and a Korean court-martial, not a broken infrastructure. The Highlands and their peoples were reached by neither provision: the task force had no files on the Montagnard hamlets and no Montagnard-speaking interrogators, and FULRO's quarrel with Saigon was outside both provisions' reach. The war's end is memorandum 011's: the Republic stands at the end of 1975 in III and IV Corps and the southern coast, with its fall expected in the dry season of 1976.

## §4.N Net assessment and end state

**The end state does not change.** The Republic of Vietnam survives 1975 in III and IV Corps and on the coast south of Nha Trang, under Thieu, without American air power, facing five PAVN corps. Its fall is expected between February and May 1976. The rung is (b), dependent survival, by the test below, because Saigon stands past 30 April 1975. The outcome in its own terms is the one memorandum 011 named: a reprieve of about a year, which the record ends before it is spent.

**Why this memorandum does not move it.** The two provisions act on the war while the Americans are present. The combined command's power was American power, delegated through a Vietnamese deputy and exercised over corps commanders the palace chose, and its American half and its fires went home with the American forces. The task force's speed, lift and raid arm were American and allied, and they went home too. What stayed was a Vietnamese Special Branch with ten years of files and a district layer of the infrastructure broken in the provinces the Republic kept. That is real, and the record already shows it at work in 1973. But the war's end was not decided in the hamlets of III and IV Corps. It was decided at Ban Me Thuot, on Route 7B, on the coast road out of Hue and in Congress. Neither provision reaches any of those. This is the record's oldest position, stated again: no provision that leaves the Saigon government's standing in the countryside unchanged moves the end state above rung (b). The task force weakens the Front's government without strengthening Saigon's, and the combined command strengthens the ARVN under American command, which is the opposite of what the end state needs.

**Why the next rung is not reachable.** Rung (c) requires the GVN and RVNAF to hold the countryside in every corps region without American ground forces, and American air power available at the end. Neither holds. The coast of I and II Corps fell to PAVN divisions in 1972 and 1975, and Case–Church ended American air power in 1973. Nothing in this memorandum changes either. The task force's thinnest work was on the coast, and the combined command is an American command that ends with the American air.

**The date of the fall.** The window stays February to May 1976. Two mechanisms in this memorandum pull it earlier. The first is the counter-directional finding: an ARVN that planned inside a combined staff for nine years has a weaker staff of its own in 1975. The record shows the Delta transfer stopping at the palace's corps commander. The second is the national file, a better instrument for the palace's purges of 1970–71. Two pull it later. The Front's infrastructure in III and IV Corps is weaker than in any earlier version of this war, so the PAVN's offensive on the capital will have fewer guides, caches and recruiting grounds in the provinces it must cross, as the Hoc Mon columns had at Tet. And the Special Branch's files are a better instrument against the Front's last-hour risings in the cities. I judge the two pairs roughly to cancel, because the first acts on the ARVN's command in a battle and the second on the enemy's rear, and the 1976 offensive will be decided by the first. The window does not move, and this is a conclusion of the test, not a carry.

**Running sums.** American lives saved: the record's sum stands at about 3,700–7,800. This memorandum adds about 50–300 in 1964–68, chiefly at Hue (about 15, EV000-44) and in the cross-boundary blocks of 1966–67, at a rate of perhaps a fraction of one percent of the American dead of those years. The sum moves to about 3,750–8,100. It supports the lower half of a trace on American casualties and does not move tolerance. ARVN battalions not broken: this memorandum adds none that I can show in a dated event, and I do not claim any. The infrastructure is the variable this memorandum moves, and its sum is the phase vectors above. In 1965–68 the record now holds the Front's district layer at about a third to a half weaker in 12–18 provinces than before this memorandum, a product of overlapping judgments whose upper bound I mark: the half is the upper bound, and the third is what I would defend¹⁵. The enemy's answer, which DIRCP02-3 requires me to name, was to move the district committees to the base areas and govern from further out.

**The four regions.**
- **The Delta:** a Vietnamese-commanded combined corps from July 1966 that executed cautiously and held the territorial battalions in their districts.
- **The coastal lowlands of I and II Corps:** a list, a killed family at Hoa Thanh and a story the Front still told in 1968, and Hue as one battle.
- **The Highlands and their peoples:** nothing. The task force had no files and no language there, and the combined command's Highlands war was the American divisions' frontier war, unchanged.
- **The Saigon belt:** the strongest effect, from Can Giuoc in 1964 to Hoc Mon at Tet.

**The soldier's family.** The combined command moved ARVN divisions across corps boundaries more readily than history did, beginning with the 21st Division's sweep into Ca Mau in March 1964. An ARVN soldier's family lived beside his unit's base. A division moved across a corps line left its families behind or brought them into the new corps area's camps, and either way its desertion rose for the months of the move⁸⁸. The one-in-three rule kept most territorial-security battalions in their provinces and their families' reach, which is the rule's least advertised value.

**Rice and the piaster.** The task force's work against the region finance section is a work on rice and money. The ledger of Phu Hoa Dong recorded the Front's collections in piasters and in rice, hamlet by hamlet, across Hoc Mon, Go Vap, Cu Chi and Trang Bang. Its collectors were taken in the winter of 1965–66, and the region's take from the market gardens and the rubber workers fell accordingly. The Front's rice in the Saigon belt in 1966–67 came increasingly from purchase in the towns and from Cambodia rather than from tax in the hamlets. That is a cost the Front paid in money it had to bring from the North¹⁵. The piaster and the soldier's pay are untouched by either provision.

**The enemy's authors.** The decisions in this memorandum's events were taken by different bodies. On the coast it was Military Region 5's Party Committee, against the B3 Front. In Hoc Mon it was the Saigon–Gia Dinh Region, under COSVN, carrying out Hanoi's plan for Tet. The combined staff's agent belonged to the region's military intelligence section. Hanoi's Politburo appears in none of them except as the author of the Tet plan, which is as it should be: this memorandum's war is the war below the Politburo.

**Phase redraw.** No redraw is proposed. Neither provision changes the shape of the phases.

**A question for the record.** The chapters of Phases IV to VI carry no event for the combined command's handover or dissolution, or for the task force's drawdown, and this memorandum has defended their absence as nulls. The next checkpoint should decide whether the combined command's end in 1972–73, when the last American corps-level headquarters left, needs an event of its own.

- b1 · PASS · The Republic survived Tet in 1968 (EV000-42, EV000-43, EV000-44, the last now one battle under Truong ended 27 February) and the Easter Offensive of 1972, the offensives fought while US forces were in the country
- b2 · PASS · Saigon stands past 30 April 1975: the assault was put back to the dry season of 1975–76, and the Republic holds III and IV Corps on 31 December 1975; nothing in this memorandum alters the critical path of 1975 (null P6)
- c1 · PASS · At An Loc and Kontum in April–June 1972 the RVNAF defeated the PAVN's conventional offensive with American air power, advisers and materiel and no American ground combat units in the fight; true historically as well
- c2 · FAIL · The insurgency is not contained in every corps region: the task force's work on the II Corps coast ended in the abandoned list of 1966, northern Binh Dinh fell to the PAVN in 1972, and I Corps and the Highlands are lost in 1975
- c3 · FAIL · Saigon stands at the end of 1975, but no American air power is available to it: Case–Church ended American combat in Indochina on 15 August 1973 and Congress refused the President's request in January 1975
- d1 · FAIL · No dated event shows the RVNAF defeating a conventional offensive on materiel alone: Phuoc Long fell, the counter-attack from Phuoc An broke, Pleiku held three weeks and was given up
- d2 · FAIL · The VCI is weaker in III and IV Corps than in any earlier version of this war (P2 and P3 VCI.strength −8..−6; EV012-4) but governs the lost provinces and is still hunted in the held belt (P6 VCI.strength −6..−3); not reduced to banditry anywhere
- d3 · PASS · Literally true: Saigon stands at the end of the record, 31 December 1975, with no US combat forces in or over the country; the condition holds only because the record ends before the offensive of 1976
- e1 · FAIL · Hanoi's resolution of October 1975 sets the general offensive on Saigon for the first months of 1976
- e2 · FAIL · P6 HANOI.will 0..0: the delay was a matter of calendar, not of will

## §5 Delta ledger

**Forces gained.**
- P012a, the Combined Operations Command, Vietnam, from 1 January 1964: a combined headquarters and four corps-area combined centres, about 1,400–2,000 staff at full strength. It holds operational control of the ARVN corps and divisions, the ROK forces (with a countersignature and national referral) and the Australian task force (with a casualty referral to Canberra).
- P012b, the Combined Joint Interagency Task Force against the VCI, from 1 January 1964: about 2,500–3,000 at full strength, a national fusion centre, four corps task groups, 44 province cells, about 1,000–1,200 raid and arrest troops and 20–30 dedicated helicopters. Its II Corps Korean element was withdrawn on 2 April 1966.

**Events.** Eight new: EV012-1 to EV012-8. One altered: EV000-44, the battle of Hue, now ending 27 February 1968.

**Variables moved (end vectors).**
- P1: VCI.strength converted −4..−2 → −5..−3 (medium); FIND.vci converted +5..+7 → +4..+7 (medium); GVN.political_stability converted 0 → −1..+1 (low).
- P2: VCI.strength converted −7..−5 → −8..−6 (medium); FIND.vci converted +8..+10 → +6..+9 (medium); ENEMY.engagement_control converted 0 → −1..0 (low); ARVN.self_sufficiency.fire_support −1..+1 → −2..+1 (low).
- P3: VCI.strength converted −7..−5 → −8..−6 (medium); FIND.vci converted +8..+10 → +6..+9 (medium); ARVN.self_sufficiency.fire_support −1..+1 → −2..+1 (low).
- P4–P6: unchanged (stated nulls).

**New categories.** A combined command of the war in the South, with operational control of the RVNAF by an American commander, a first in the record. A dedicated finishing force against the infrastructure, distinct from the district centres. The two-layer plan as a standing practice of the combined staff.

**End state.** Unchanged: rung (b), fall expected February–May 1976.

## §6 Changed / not changed

### What has changed

- **The Front's district government of the held belt was broken from its edge in 1965–67.** The task force reached the committees that memorandum 006's centres had driven into the contested edge. The Saigon–Gia Dinh Region's collections from Hoc Mon and Go Vap fell to about an eighth of 1964's, and the Front governed Hoc Mon from the Ho Bo woods. This is the largest single blow to the infrastructure in the record, about a third to a half of the district layer in 12–18 provinces beyond what the record held¹⁵, and it is the half of the war the American divisions could never touch.
- **The Can Giuoc security section was broken in 1964,** and the land programme returned to Can Giuoc's posted hamlets two months early with no title holder killed in 1965. The task force protected the land reform where it had first been shown helpless.
- **The war had one military commander from 1964.** Combined operational control closed seams between national sectors, made an ARVN division movable across a corps line in a day, and made Hue one battle that ended five days early with about 55 fewer allied dead.
- **The Korean and ARVN sectors on the II Corps coast were planned by one staff** instead of negotiated by two.
- **A Vietnamese general commanded a combined corps area from July 1966,** with an American as his deputy, the first time in the war.
- **At Tet the northwestern columns came through Hoc Mon with nobody to guide them** and reached Tan Son Nhut late. It was a small effect, and the proof that the infrastructure war of 1965 shaped the main-force war of 1968.

### What has not changed

- **The main force is found no better and still chooses its battles.** The combined command shortened the fix and closed seams. The enemy stopped using the seams and fought at the borders, and its sanctuaries were untouched.
- **The coast and the Highlands.** The task force failed on the II Corps coast, where its list killed a family and its Korean element was withdrawn. It never reached the Montagnard hamlets.
- **The combined command could not choose corps commanders.** The Delta transfer reached Dang Van Quang and Nguyen Van Manh, the palace's choices, and stopped. The ARVN's corps and divisions are no better led than they were.
- **The combined staff was penetrated,** as its dossier said it would be, for two and a half years.
- **The American lever lapses in 1970–71, and with it the protection of both provisions.** The task force's file goes where memorandum 006's went, into the President's service.
- **The end of the war is unchanged.** The Republic stands at the end of 1975 in III and IV Corps, without American air power, and its fall is expected in the dry season of 1976. Neither provision reaches Hanoi's will, the sanctuaries, the PAVN's divisions, the orders of March 1975 or the American Congress.

### Trajectory Log entry

Memorandum 012 gave the war a combined command and a finishing arm against the infrastructure, and the second mattered more. The combined command survived Khanh's coup by passing its orders through his office. It closed seams the enemy then stopped using, and made Hue one battle that ended five days early. Its transfer to Vietnamese command began in the Delta in 1966 and stopped at the palace's corps commanders, and an agent sat in its plans cell for two and a half years. The task force broke the Can Giuoc security section in 1964 and, in FOLLOW THROUGH, the Saigon–Gia Dinh Region's tax administration west of Saigon. At Tet the attackers crossed Hoc Mon with no one to guide them. On the coast its list killed a family and was abandoned. The end state does not move: rung (b), with the Republic's fall expected in 1976.

### Positions recorded

- **POS012-1** · vars: VCI.strength, FIND.vci, POP.night_control · In this war a combined interagency task force with lift, raid teams and cross-province warrants from 1964 broke the Front's district government of the held belt from its edge in 1965–67, about a third to a half of the district layer in 12–18 provinces beyond what the police and the district centres had done; it reached committees beyond the held belt only as far as helicopter and warrant, never into the base areas, and its gains hold only where the posts hold the night (012 F012-27; EV012-4)
- **POS012-2** · vars: ADVISE.leverage, ARVN.leadership, GVN.political_stability · In this war COMUSMACV held operational control of the ARVN corps from 1964 as combined commander, exercised through the prime minister's office after Khanh's coup and over corps commanders the palace chose; it was more than coordination and less than command, and it ended in fact with the American divisions and the American lever (012 EV012-1, EV012-6)
- **POS012-3** · vars: INTL.allied_forces, POP.civilian_harm · In this war the Korean forces accepted combined operational control only with a countersignature and a national referral, which put Korean soldiers beyond the Republic's law; the Korean element of the task force was withdrawn after Hoa Thanh in April 1966 and Korean soldiers took no further part in targeting the infrastructure (012 EV012-3, EV012-5)

### Persons recorded

- **Thomas R. Garvin** · Major, US Army Special Forces, commander of the task force's III Corps task group 1964–66 · invented in 012 · Ran DRY SEASON, which found nothing for two months and then broke the Can Giuoc security section, and FOLLOW THROUGH, which took the region finance section's ledger at Phu Hoa Dong
- **Nguyen Huu Phuoc** · Captain, then Lieutenant Colonel, Police Special Branch, deputy of the III Corps task group 1964–66 · invented in 012 · Received Ba Lien at Tan An at 0710 on 6 October 1964; held the Special Branch's veto over the task group's raids
- **Harold J. Beck** · Lieutenant Colonel, US Army, commander of the task force's II Corps task group 1965–66 · invented in 012 · Adopted the 64-name list on the II Corps coast, which killed a family at Hoa Thanh and was ended on 2 April 1966
- **Nguyen Van Tu** · Secretary of the Hoc Mon district committee · invented in 012 · Escaped STILL WATER and FOLLOW THROUGH and governed Hoc Mon from the Ho Bo woods from January 1966

Memorandum 012 (a combined US, Vietnamese, Korean and Australian operations command from 1 January 1964, P012a, and a combined task force against the infrastructure, P012b) touches the air war in the South through one mechanism: the combined fire-support centres, which route American air, Arc Light and the B-52J's close-support boxes to ARVN units through one switchboard by plan rather than by an adviser's request. It shows at work in one dated event. At Hue in February 1968 a single fire centre in the Citadel took every call for naval gunfire, artillery and the aircraft the weather allowed, and the battle ended on 27 February rather than 2 March. The memorandum widens the Republic's own fire support to −2..+1 in Phases II and III, on the finding that the switchboard makes American fire more available to the ARVN and no more owned. It leaves *ARVN.effectiveness.fire_coordination* unjudged for the reason the study gave (Q-5). Its night raids push *POP.civilian_harm* (0..+3), which remains without a holder (Q-6). It changes nothing over the North, on the Trail or in the VNAF.
